Question 1 · Basic String Operations · Easy

Reverse a String

Given a string, reverse it and return the result.

Example 1

Input: "hello"

Output: "olleh"

Kotlin

Basic Solution Best

Best: this is the cleanest and most direct version, and it is usually the strongest first answer in an interview.

fun reverseAString(input: String): String {
    return input.reversed()
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Alternative Good to try

Good to try: this is a valid backup and easy to explain, but the simplest direct solution usually reads better in an interview.

fun reverseAString(input: String): String {
    val charArray = input.toCharArray()
    val result = StringBuilder()
    for (i in charArray.size - 1 downTo 0) {
        result.append(charArray[i])
    }
    return result.toString()

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

fun reverseAString(input: String): String {
    val charArray = input.toCharArray()
    val reversed = CharArray(charArray.size)
    for (i in charArray.indices) {
        reversed[charArray.size - 1 - i] = charArray[i]
    }
    return String(reversed)

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Stream API Good to try

Good to try: this is concise and elegant, but it is usually a second-choice answer unless the problem is clearly built for a collection pipeline.

fun reverseAString(input: String): String {
    return input.asSequence().reversed().joinToString("")
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Java

Basic Solution Best

Best: this is the cleanest and most direct version, and it is usually the strongest first answer in an interview.

public static String reverseAString(String input) {
    return new StringBuilder(input).reverse().toString();
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Alternative Good to try

Good to try: this is a valid backup and easy to explain, but the simplest direct solution usually reads better in an interview.

public static String reverseAString(String input) {
    char[] chars = input.toCharArray();
    int left = 0;
    int right = chars.length - 1;
    while (left < right) {
        char temp = chars[left];
        chars[left] = chars[right];
        chars[right] = temp;
        left++;
        right--;
    }
    return new String(chars);

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

public static String reverseAString(String input) {
    char[] chars = input.toCharArray();
    char[] reversed = new char[chars.length];
    for (int i = 0; i < chars.length; i++) {
        reversed[chars.length - 1 - i] = chars[i];
    }
    return new String(reversed);

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Stream API Good to try

Good to try: this is concise and elegant, but it is usually a second-choice answer unless the problem is clearly built for a collection pipeline.

public static String reverseAString(String input) {
    return IntStream.range(0, input.length())
        .mapToObj(index -> String.valueOf(input.charAt(input.length() - 1 - index)))
        .collect(Collectors.joining());

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Question 2 · Basic String Operations · Easy

Check if String is Palindrome

Determine whether a string reads the same forward and backward.

Example 1

Input: "level"

Output: true

Kotlin

Basic Solution Best

Best: this is the cleanest and most direct version, and it is usually the strongest first answer in an interview.

fun checkIfStringIsPalindrome(input: String): Boolean {
    return input == input.reversed()
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Alternative Good to try

Good to try: this is a valid backup and easy to explain, but the simplest direct solution usually reads better in an interview.

fun checkIfStringIsPalindrome(input: String): Boolean {
    var left = 0
    var right = input.length - 1
    while (left < right) {
        if (input[left] != input[right]) return false
        left++
        right--
    }
    return true

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(1)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

fun checkIfStringIsPalindrome(input: String): Boolean {
    val chars = input.toCharArray()
    for (i in chars.indices) {
        if (chars[i] != chars[chars.size - 1 - i]) return false
    }
    return true

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Stream API Best

Good to try: this version is compact and readable, although a simpler loop or direct implementation is often the stronger first answer.

fun checkIfStringIsPalindrome(input: String): Boolean {
    return input.filter { it.isLetterOrDigit() }.lowercase() == input.filter { it.isLetterOrDigit() }.lowercase().reversed()
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Java

Basic Solution Best

Best: this is the cleanest and most direct version, and it is usually the strongest first answer in an interview.

public static boolean checkIfStringIsPalindrome(String input) {
    return input.equals(new StringBuilder(input).reverse().toString());
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Alternative Good to try

Good to try: this is a valid backup and easy to explain, but the simplest direct solution usually reads better in an interview.

public static boolean checkIfStringIsPalindrome(String input) {
    int left = 0;
    int right = input.length() - 1;
    while (left < right) {
        if (input.charAt(left) != input.charAt(right)) return false;
        left++;
        right--;
    }
    return true;

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(1)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

public static boolean checkIfStringIsPalindrome(String input) {
    char[] chars = input.toCharArray();
    for (int i = 0; i < chars.length; i++) {
        if (chars[i] != chars[chars.length - 1 - i]) return false;
    }
    return true;

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Stream API Best

Good to try: this version is compact and readable, although a simpler loop or direct implementation is often the stronger first answer.

public static boolean checkIfStringIsPalindrome(String input) {
    String normalized = input.replaceAll("[^a-zA-Z0-9]", "").toLowerCase();
    return normalized.equals(new StringBuilder(normalized).reverse().toString());

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Question 3 · Basic String Operations · Easy

Count vowels in a String

Count the number of vowels in a string.

Example 1

Input: "interview"

Output: 4

Kotlin

Basic Solution Best

Best: this is the cleanest and most direct version, and it is usually the strongest first answer in an interview.

fun countVowelsInAString(input: String): Int {
    return input.count { it.lowercaseChar() in setOf('a', 'e', 'i', 'o', 'u') }
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Alternative Best

Best: this keeps the core idea simple and readable without overcomplicating the logic.

fun countVowelsInAString(input: String): Int {
    val vowels = setOf('a', 'e', 'i', 'o', 'u')
    return input.sumOf { if (it.lowercaseChar() in vowels) 1 else 0 }
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

fun countVowelsInAString(input: String): Int {
    var count = 0
    for (char in input) {
        if (char.lowercaseChar() in "aeiou") count++
    }
    return count
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(1)

Stream API Good to try

Good to try: this is concise and elegant, but it is usually a second-choice answer unless the problem is clearly built for a collection pipeline.

fun countVowelsInAString(input: String): Int {
    return input.asSequence().count { it.lowercaseChar() in "aeiou" }
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(1)

Java

Basic Solution Best

Best: this is the cleanest and most direct version, and it is usually the strongest first answer in an interview.

public static String countVowelsInAString(String input) { return input.trim(); }

Time: O(n)   Space: O(1)

Alternative Best

Best: this keeps the core idea simple and readable without overcomplicating the logic.

public static String countVowelsInAString(String input) { return input.trim(); }

Time: O(n)   Space: O(1)

Loop Best

Best: this is the clearest control-flow version and is a reliable fallback if the interviewer prefers explicit logic.

public static String countVowelsInAString(String input) { return input.trim(); }

Time: O(n)   Space: O(1)

Stream API Best

Good to try: this version is compact and readable, although a simpler loop or direct implementation is often the stronger first answer.

public static String countVowelsInAString(String input) { return input.trim(); }

Time: O(n)   Space: O(1)

Question 4 · Basic String Operations · Easy

Count each character in a String

Input: "banana". Output: b1a3n2, with each character followed by its count in first-appearance order.

Example 1

Input: "banana"

Output: b1a3n2

Kotlin

String Output (Loop) Best

Keeps characters in first-seen order and returns each character followed by its count, such as banana -> b1a3n2.

fun charCount(input: String): String {
    val counts = linkedMapOf<Char, Int>()
    for (char in input) {
        counts[char] = (counts[char] ?: 0) + 1
    }
    return counts.entries.joinToString("") { "${it.key}${it.value}" }
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(1)

String Output (groupingBy) Good to try

A concise groupingBy version that returns characters and counts in first-seen order.

fun charCount(input: String): String =
    input.groupingBy { it }.eachCount()
        .entries.joinToString("") { "${it.key}${it.value}" }

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Basic Solution Good to try

Good to try: this is readable and compact, but the stream chain is usually less explicit than the cleaner loop-based explanation when the interviewer wants exact control.

fun countEachCharacterInAString(input: String): Map<Char, Int> {
    return input.groupingBy { it }.eachCount()
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Alternative Good to try

Good to try: this is a valid backup and easy to explain, but the simplest direct solution usually reads better in an interview.

fun countEachCharacterInAString(input: String): Map<Char, Int> {
    val counts = mutableMapOf<Char, Int>()
    for (char in input) {
        counts[char] = counts.getOrDefault(char, 0) + 1
    }
    return counts

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

fun countEachCharacterInAString(input: String): Map<Char, Int> {
    val counts = mutableMapOf<Char, Int>()
    for (char in input) {
        if (counts.containsKey(char)) {
            counts[char] = counts[char]!! + 1
        } else {
            counts[char] = 1
        }
    }
    return counts

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Stream API Good to try

Good to try: this is concise and elegant, but it is usually a second-choice answer unless the problem is clearly built for a collection pipeline.

fun countEachCharacterInAString(input: String): Map<Char, Int> {
    return input.asSequence().groupingBy { it }.eachCount()
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Java

Basic Solution Avoid unless needed

Good to try: this is readable and compact, but the stream chain is usually less explicit than the cleaner loop-based explanation when the interviewer wants exact control.

public static Map<Character, Integer> countEachCharacterInAString(String input) {
    return input.chars().mapToObj(c -> (char) c).collect(Collectors.groupingBy(c -> c, LinkedHashMap::new, Collectors.summingInt(c -> 1)));
 }

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Alternative Avoid unless needed

Best: this keeps the core idea simple and readable without overcomplicating the logic.

public static Map<Character, Integer> countEachCharacterInAString(String input) {
    return input.chars().mapToObj(c -> (char) c).collect(Collectors.groupingBy(c -> c, LinkedHashMap::new, Collectors.summingInt(c -> 1)));
 }

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Loop Avoid unless needed

Best: this is the clearest control-flow version and is a reliable fallback if the interviewer prefers explicit logic.

public static Map<Character, Integer> countEachCharacterInAString(String input) {
    return input.chars().mapToObj(c -> (char) c).collect(Collectors.groupingBy(c -> c, LinkedHashMap::new, Collectors.summingInt(c -> 1)));
 }

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Stream API Avoid unless needed

Avoid unless needed: this flatMap/flatten style is clever but often harder to explain and reason about than a straightforward loop or direct collection approach.

public static Map<Character, Integer> countEachCharacterInAString(String input) {
    return input.chars().mapToObj(c -> (char) c).collect(Collectors.groupingBy(c -> c, LinkedHashMap::new, Collectors.summingInt(c -> 1)));
 }

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Question 5 · Basic String Operations · Easy

Find duplicate characters

Return the duplicate characters found in a string.

Example 1

Input: "programming"

Output: [r, g, m]

Kotlin

Basic Solution Best

Best: this is the cleanest and most direct version, and it is usually the strongest first answer in an interview.

fun findDuplicateCharacters(input: String): List<Char> {
    val seen = mutableSetOf<Char>()
    val duplicates = mutableListOf<Char>()
    for (char in input) {
        if (!seen.add(char) && char !in duplicates) duplicates.add(char)
    }
    return duplicates

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Alternative Best

Best: this keeps the core idea simple and readable without overcomplicating the logic.

fun findDuplicateCharacters(input: String): List<Char> {
    return input.groupingBy { it }.eachCount().filterValues { it > 1 }.keys.toList()
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

fun findDuplicateCharacters(input: String): List<Char> {
    val counts = mutableMapOf<Char, Int>()
    for (char in input) counts[char] = (counts[char] ?: 0) + 1
    return input.filter { counts[it]!! > 1 }.distinct()

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Stream API Good to try

Good to try: this is concise and elegant, but it is usually a second-choice answer unless the problem is clearly built for a collection pipeline.

fun findDuplicateCharacters(input: String): List<Char> {
    return input.asSequence().groupingBy { it }.eachCount().filterValues { it > 1 }.keys.toList()
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Java

Basic Solution Best

Best: this is the cleanest and most direct version, and it is usually the strongest first answer in an interview.

public static List<Character> findDuplicateCharacters(String input) {
    Set<Character> seen = new HashSet<>();
    List<Character> duplicates = new ArrayList<>();
    for (char c : input.toCharArray()) {
        if (!seen.add(c) && !duplicates.contains(c)) duplicates.add(c);
    }
    return duplicates;

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Alternative Good to try

Good to try: this is a valid backup and easy to explain, but the simplest direct solution usually reads better in an interview.

public static List<Character> findDuplicateCharacters(String input) {
    Map<Character, Integer> counts = new LinkedHashMap<>();
    for (char c : input.toCharArray()) counts.put(c, counts.getOrDefault(c, 0) + 1);
    List<Character> duplicates = new ArrayList<>();
    for (char c : input.toCharArray()) if (counts.get(c) > 1 && !duplicates.contains(c)) duplicates.add(c);
    return duplicates;

}

Time: O(n^2)   Space: O(n)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

public static List<Character> findDuplicateCharacters(String input) {
    List<Character> duplicates = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < input.length(); i++) {
        char c = input.charAt(i);
        if (input.indexOf(c) != input.lastIndexOf(c) && !duplicates.contains(c)) duplicates.add(c);
    }
    return duplicates;

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Stream API Avoid unless needed

Avoid unless needed: this flatMap/flatten style is clever but often harder to explain and reason about than a straightforward loop or direct collection approach.

public static List<Character> findDuplicateCharacters(String input) {
    Map<Character, Long> counts = input.chars().mapToObj(c -> (char) c).collect(Collectors.groupingBy(c -> c, LinkedHashMap::new, Collectors.counting()));
    return input.chars().mapToObj(c -> (char) c).filter(c -> counts.get(c) > 1).distinct().collect(Collectors.toList());

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Question 6 · Basic String Operations · Medium

Find first non-repeating character

Return the first character that does not repeat.

Example 1

Input: "swiss"

Output: w

Kotlin

Basic Solution Good to try

Good to try: this is readable and compact, but the stream chain is usually less explicit than the cleaner loop-based explanation when the interviewer wants exact control.

fun findFirstNonRepeatingCharacter(input: String): Char {
    return input.groupingBy { it }.eachCount().let { counts -> input.first { counts[it] == 1 } }
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Alternative Best

Best: this keeps the core idea simple and readable without overcomplicating the logic.

fun findFirstNonRepeatingCharacter(input: String): Char {
    val counts = input.groupingBy { it }.eachCount()
    return input.firstOrNull { counts[it] == 1 } ?: '\u0000'
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

fun findFirstNonRepeatingCharacter(input: String): Char {
    val counts = mutableMapOf<Char, Int>()
    for (char in input) counts[char] = (counts[char] ?: 0) + 1
    for (char in input) if (counts[char] == 1) return char
    return '\u0000'

}

Time: O(n^2)   Space: O(n)

Stream API Good to try

Good to try: this is concise and elegant, but it is usually a second-choice answer unless the problem is clearly built for a collection pipeline.

fun findFirstNonRepeatingCharacter(input: String): Char {
    return input.asSequence().groupingBy { it }.eachCount().let { counts -> input.first { counts[it] == 1 } }
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Java

Basic Solution Good to try

Good to try: this is readable and compact, but the stream chain is usually less explicit than the cleaner loop-based explanation when the interviewer wants exact control.

public static List<Character> findFirstNonRepeatingCharacter(String input) { return input.chars().mapToObj(c -> (char) c).distinct().collect(Collectors.toList()); }

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Alternative Best

Best: this keeps the core idea simple and readable without overcomplicating the logic.

public static List<Character> findFirstNonRepeatingCharacter(String input) { return input.chars().mapToObj(c -> (char) c).distinct().collect(Collectors.toList()); }

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Loop Best

Best: this is the clearest control-flow version and is a reliable fallback if the interviewer prefers explicit logic.

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Good to try: this is concise and elegant, but it is usually a second-choice answer unless the problem is clearly built for a collection pipeline.

Question 7 · Basic String Operations · Easy

Find first repeating character

Identify the first character that repeats.

Example 1

Input: "swiss"

Output: s

Kotlin

Basic Solution Good to try

Good to try: this is readable and compact, but the stream chain is usually less explicit than the cleaner loop-based explanation when the interviewer wants exact control.

fun findFirstRepeatingCharacter(input: String): Char {
    return input.groupingBy { it }.eachCount().let { counts -> input.first { counts[it]!! > 1 } }
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Alternative Good to try

Good to try: this is a valid backup and easy to explain, but the simplest direct solution usually reads better in an interview.

fun findFirstRepeatingCharacter(input: String): Char {
    val seen = mutableSetOf<Char>()
    for (char in input) if (!seen.add(char)) return char
    return '\u0000'
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

fun findFirstRepeatingCharacter(input: String): Char {
    val seen = mutableSetOf<Char>()
    for (char in input) {
        if (char in seen) return char
        seen.add(char)
    }
    return '\u0000'

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Stream API Good to try

Good to try: this is concise and elegant, but it is usually a second-choice answer unless the problem is clearly built for a collection pipeline.

fun findFirstRepeatingCharacter(input: String): Char {
    return input.asSequence().groupingBy { it }.eachCount().let { counts -> input.first { counts[it]!! > 1 } }
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Java

Basic Solution Good to try

Good to try: this is readable and compact, but the stream chain is usually less explicit than the cleaner loop-based explanation when the interviewer wants exact control.

public static List<Character> findFirstRepeatingCharacter(String input) { return input.chars().mapToObj(c -> (char) c).distinct().collect(Collectors.toList()); }

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Alternative Best

Best: this keeps the core idea simple and readable without overcomplicating the logic.

Question 8 · Basic String Operations · Easy

Remove duplicate characters

Output: "progamin"

Kotlin

Basic Solution Best

Best: this is the cleanest and most direct version, and it is usually the strongest first answer in an interview.

}

Best: this keeps the core idea simple and readable without overcomplicating the logic.

    return input.filter { seen.add(it) }
}

fun removeDuplicateCharacters(input: String): String {

    return result.toString()

}

public static String removeDuplicateCharacters(String input) {
    Set<Character> seen = new LinkedHashSet<>();
    for (char c : input.toCharArray()) seen.add(c);
    StringBuilder result = new StringBuilder();
    for (char c : seen) result.append(c);
    return result.toString();

}

Time: O(n^2)   Space: O(n)

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

    return result.toString();

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Best: this keeps the core idea simple and readable without overcomplicating the logic.

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

fun countWordsInASentence(input: String): Int {

    }
    return count

}

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

Good to try: this is concise and elegant, but it is usually a second-choice answer unless the problem is clearly built for a collection pipeline.

Question 10 · Basic String Operations · Medium

Reverse each word in a sentence

Reverse every word in a sentence while preserving the order of words.

Example 1

Input: "hello world"

Output: "olleh dlrow"

Kotlin

Basic Solution Best

Best: this is the cleanest and most direct version, and it is usually the strongest first answer in an interview.

fun reverseEachWordInASentence(input: String): String {
    return input.reversed()
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Alternative Good to try

Good to try: this is a valid backup and easy to explain, but the simplest direct solution usually reads better in an interview.

fun reverseEachWordInASentence(input: String): String {
    val chars = input.toCharArray()
    val result = StringBuilder()
    for (index in chars.indices.reversed()) {
        result.append(chars[index])
    }
    return result.toString()

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

fun reverseEachWordInASentence(input: String): String {
    val chars = input.toCharArray()
    val reversed = CharArray(chars.size)
    for (i in chars.indices) {
        reversed[chars.size - 1 - i] = chars[i]
    }
    return String(reversed)

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Stream API Good to try

Good to try: this is concise and elegant, but it is usually a second-choice answer unless the problem is clearly built for a collection pipeline.

fun reverseEachWordInASentence(input: String): String {
    return input.asSequence().reversed().joinToString("")
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Java

Basic Solution Best

Best: this is the cleanest and most direct version, and it is usually the strongest first answer in an interview.

public static String reverseEachWordInASentence(String input) {
    return new StringBuilder(input).reverse().toString();
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Alternative Good to try

Good to try: this is a valid backup and easy to explain, but the simplest direct solution usually reads better in an interview.

public static String reverseEachWordInASentence(String input) {
    char[] chars = input.toCharArray();
    int left = 0;
    int right = chars.length - 1;
    while (left < right) {
        char temp = chars[left];
        chars[left] = chars[right];
        chars[right] = temp;
        left++;
        right--;
    }
    return new String(chars);

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

public static String reverseEachWordInASentence(String input) {
    char[] chars = input.toCharArray();
    char[] reversed = new char[chars.length];
    for (int i = 0; i < chars.length; i++) {
        reversed[chars.length - 1 - i] = chars[i];
    }
    return new String(reversed);

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Stream API Good to try

Good to try: this is concise and elegant, but it is usually a second-choice answer unless the problem is clearly built for a collection pipeline.

public static String reverseEachWordInASentence(String input) {
    return IntStream.range(0, input.length())
        .mapToObj(index -> String.valueOf(input.charAt(input.length() - 1 - index)))
        .collect(Collectors.joining());

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Question 11 · Basic String Operations · Easy

Find the longest word

Return the longest word among a list of words.

Example 1

Input: ["cat", "elephant", "dog"]

Output: "elephant"

Kotlin

Basic Solution Best

Best: this is the cleanest and most direct version, and it is usually the strongest first answer in an interview.

fun findTheLongestWord(input: String): String {
    return input.split(Regex("\s+")).maxByOrNull { it.length } ?: ""
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Alternative Best

Best: this keeps the core idea simple and readable without overcomplicating the logic.

fun findTheLongestWord(input: String): String {
      val words = input.split(Regex("\s+"))
      return words.maxByOrNull { it.length } ?: ""
    }

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

fun findTheLongestWord(input: String): String {
    var longest = ""
      for (word in input.split(Regex("\s+"))) {
        if (word.length > longest.length) longest = word
      }
      return longest

    }

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Stream API Best

Good to try: this version is compact and readable, although a simpler loop or direct implementation is often the stronger first answer.

fun findTheLongestWord(input: String): String {
    return input.split(Regex("\s+")).maxByOrNull { it.length } ?: ""
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Java

Basic Solution Good to try

Good to try: this is readable and compact, but the stream chain is usually less explicit than the cleaner loop-based explanation when the interviewer wants exact control.

public static String findTheLongestWord(String input) { return Arrays.stream(input.split("\s+")).max(Comparator.comparingInt(String::length)).orElse(""); }

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Alternative Good to try

Good to try: this is a valid backup and easy to explain, but the simplest direct solution usually reads better in an interview.

public static String findTheLongestWord(String input) {
    String[] words = input.split("\s+"); String longest = ""; for (String word : words) if (word.length() > longest.length()) longest = word; return longest;
 }

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Loop Avoid unless needed

Best: this is the clearest control-flow version and is a reliable fallback if the interviewer prefers explicit logic.

public static String findTheLongestWord(String input) {
    return Arrays.stream(input.split("\s+")).reduce((longest, current) -> current.length() > longest.length() ? current : longest).orElse("");
 }

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Stream API Good to try

Good to try: this is concise and elegant, but it is usually a second-choice answer unless the problem is clearly built for a collection pipeline.

public static String findTheLongestWord(String input) { return Arrays.stream(input.split("\s+")).max(Comparator.comparingInt(String::length)).orElse(""); }

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Question 16 · Arrays & Lists · Easy

Find largest number

Find the largest number present in an array.

Example 1

Input: [3, 9, 2, 7]

Output: 9

Kotlin

Basic Solution Good to try

Good to try: this is readable and compact, but the stream chain is usually less explicit than the cleaner loop-based explanation when the interviewer wants exact control.

fun findLargestNumber(input: List<Int>): Int {
    return input.maxOrNull() ?: Int.MIN_VALUE
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(1)

Alternative Good to try

Good to try: this is a valid backup and easy to explain, but the simplest direct solution usually reads better in an interview.

fun findLargestNumber(input: List<Int>): Int {
    var largest = input.firstOrNull() ?: Int.MIN_VALUE
    for (value in input.drop(1)) {
        if (value > largest) largest = value
    }
    return largest

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(1)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

fun findLargestNumber(input: List<Int>): Int {
    var largest = Int.MIN_VALUE
    for (value in input) {
        if (value > largest) largest = value
    }
    return largest
}

Stream API Good to try

Good to try: this is concise and elegant, but it is usually a second-choice answer unless the problem is clearly built for a collection pipeline.

    for (int value : input) {
        if (value > largest) largest = value;
    }
    return largest;

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(1)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

public static int findLargestNumber(List<Integer> input) {
    int largest = Integer.MIN_VALUE;
    for (int value : input) {
        if (value > largest) largest = value;
    }
    return largest;

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(1)

Good to try: this is concise and elegant, but it is usually a second-choice answer unless the problem is clearly built for a collection pipeline.

Question 17 · Arrays & Lists · Easy

Find smallest number

Find the smallest number in an array.

Example 1

Input: [3, 9, 2, 7]

Output: 2

Kotlin

Best: this is the cleanest and most direct version, and it is usually the strongest first answer in an interview.

fun findSmallestNumber(input: List<Int>): List<Int> {
    return input.filter { it > 0 }.sorted()
}

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(1)

Alternative Good to try

Good to try: this is a valid backup and easy to explain, but the simplest direct solution usually reads better in an interview.

fun findSmallestNumber(input: List<Int>): List<Int> {
    val result = mutableListOf<Int>()
    for (value in input) {
        if (value > 0) result.add(value)
    }
    result.sort()
    return result

}

Question 18 · Arrays & Lists · Medium

Find second largest number

Find the second largest value in a numeric array.

Example 1

Input: [5, 1, 9, 3]

Output: 5

Kotlin

Basic Solution Best

Best: this is the cleanest and most direct version, and it is usually the strongest first answer in an interview.

fun findSecondLargestNumber(input: List<Int>): Int {
    return input.sortedDescending().distinct()[1]
}

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(1)

Alternative Best

Best: this keeps the core idea simple and readable without overcomplicating the logic.

fun findSecondLargestNumber(input: List<Int>): Int {
    val distinct = input.distinct().sortedDescending()
    return if (distinct.size >= 2) distinct[1] else distinct.firstOrNull() ?: Int.MIN_VALUE

}

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(1)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

fun findSecondLargestNumber(input: List<Int>): Int {
    var largest = Int.MIN_VALUE
    var secondLargest = Int.MIN_VALUE
    for (value in input) {
        if (value > largest) {
            secondLargest = largest
            largest = value
        } else if (value > secondLargest && value != largest) {
            secondLargest = value
        }
    }
    return secondLargest

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(1)

Stream API Good to try

Good to try: this is concise and elegant, but it is usually a second-choice answer unless the problem is clearly built for a collection pipeline.

fun findSecondLargestNumber(input: List<Int>): Int {
    return input.asSequence().sortedDescending().distinct().drop(1).firstOrNull() ?: Int.MIN_VALUE
}

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(1)

Java

Basic Solution Good to try

Good to try: this is readable and compact, but the stream chain is usually less explicit than the cleaner loop-based explanation when the interviewer wants exact control.

Java

Basic Solution Best

Best: this is the cleanest and most direct version, and it is usually the strongest first answer in an interview.

public static String reverseAnArray(String input) {
    return new StringBuilder(input).reverse().toString();
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Alternative Good to try

Good to try: this is a valid backup and easy to explain, but the simplest direct solution usually reads better in an interview.

public static String reverseAnArray(String input) {
    char[] chars = input.toCharArray();
    int left = 0;
    int right = chars.length - 1;
    while (left < right) {
        char temp = chars[left];
        chars[left] = chars[right];
        chars[right] = temp;
        left++;
        right--;
    }
    return new String(chars);

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

public static String reverseAnArray(String input) {
    char[] chars = input.toCharArray();
    char[] reversed = new char[chars.length];
    for (int i = 0; i < chars.length; i++) {
        reversed[chars.length - 1 - i] = chars[i];
    }
    return new String(reversed);

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Stream API Good to try

Good to try: this is concise and elegant, but it is usually a second-choice answer unless the problem is clearly built for a collection pipeline.

public static String reverseAnArray(String input) {
    return IntStream.range(0, input.length())
        .mapToObj(index -> String.valueOf(input.charAt(input.length() - 1 - index)))
        .collect(Collectors.joining());

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Question 23 · Arrays & Lists · Easy

Find duplicate numbers

Find repeated values in an array.

Example 1

Input: [1, 2, 2, 3]

Output: {1=1, 2=2, 3=1}

Kotlin

Basic Solution Good to try

Good to try: this is readable and compact, but the stream chain is usually less explicit than the cleaner loop-based explanation when the interviewer wants exact control.

fun findDuplicateNumbers(input: List<Int>): Map<Int, Int> {
    return input.groupingBy { it }.eachCount()
}

Best: this keeps the core idea simple and readable without overcomplicating the logic.

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Stream API Avoid unless needed

Avoid unless needed: this flatMap/flatten style is clever but often harder to explain and reason about than a straightforward loop or direct collection approach.

Question 29 · Arrays & Lists · Medium

Move all zeros to the end

Move all zero elements to the end while preserving order.

Example 1

Input: [0, 1, 0, 3, 12]

Output: [1, 3, 12, 0, 0]

Kotlin

Basic Solution Best

Best: this is the cleanest and most direct version, and it is usually the strongest first answer in an interview.

fun moveAllZerosToTheEnd(input: List<Int>): List<Int> {
    return input.filter { it != 0 } + input.filter { it == 0 }
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(1)

Alternative Good to try

Good to try: this is a valid backup and easy to explain, but the simplest direct solution usually reads better in an interview.

fun moveAllZerosToTheEnd(input: List<Int>): List<Int> {
    val result = input.toMutableList()
    var write = 0
    for (value in result) {
        if (value != 0) {
            result[write++] = value
        }
    }
    while (write < result.size) result[write++] = 0
    return result

}

Time: O(n^2)   Space: O(1)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

fun moveAllZerosToTheEnd(input: List<Int>): List<Int> {
    val result = input.toMutableList()
    val nonZero = mutableListOf<Int>()
    val zeros = mutableListOf<Int>()
    for (value in result) {
        if (value == 0) zeros.add(value) else nonZero.add(value)
    }
    return nonZero + zeros

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Stream API Good to try

Good to try: this is concise and elegant, but it is usually a second-choice answer unless the problem is clearly built for a collection pipeline.

fun moveAllZerosToTheEnd(input: List<Int>): List<Int> {
    return input.asSequence().filter { it != 0 }.toList() + input.asSequence().filter { it == 0 }.toList()
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Java

Basic Solution Best

Best: this is the cleanest and most direct version, and it is usually the strongest first answer in an interview.

public static List<Integer> moveAllZerosToTheEnd(List<Integer> input) {
    List<Integer> result = new ArrayList<>(input);
    result.removeIf(value -> value == 0);
    while (result.size() < input.size()) result.add(0);
    return result;

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Alternative Good to try

Good to try: this is a valid backup and easy to explain, but the simplest direct solution usually reads better in an interview.

public static List<Integer> moveAllZerosToTheEnd(List<Integer> input) {
    List<Integer> result = new ArrayList<>(input);
    int write = 0;
    for (int value : result) {
        if (value != 0) {
            result.set(write++, value);
        }
    }
    while (write < result.size()) {
        result.set(write++, 0);
    }
    return result;

}

Time: O(n^2)   Space: O(n)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

public static List<Integer> moveAllZerosToTheEnd(List<Integer> input) {
    List<Integer> nonZero = new ArrayList<>();
    List<Integer> zeros = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int value : input) {
        if (value == 0) zeros.add(value);
        else nonZero.add(value);
    }
    nonZero.addAll(zeros);
    return nonZero;

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Stream API Good to try

Good to try: this is concise and elegant, but it is usually a second-choice answer unless the problem is clearly built for a collection pipeline.

public static List<Integer> moveAllZerosToTheEnd(List<Integer> input) {
    List<Integer> nonZero = input.stream().filter(value -> value != 0).collect(Collectors.toList());
    List<Integer> zeros = input.stream().filter(value -> value == 0).collect(Collectors.toList());
    nonZero.addAll(zeros);
    return nonZero;

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Question 30 · Arrays & Lists · Medium

Move all negative numbers to beginning

Place all negative numbers before positives in a single pass.

Example 1

Input: [3, -1, 2, -4]

Output: [-1, -4, 3, 2]

Kotlin

Basic Solution Best

Best: this is the cleanest and most direct version, and it is usually the strongest first answer in an interview.

fun moveAllNegativeNumbersToBeginning(input: List<Int>): List<Int> {
    return input.filter { it < 0 } + input.filter { it >= 0 }
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(1)

Alternative Good to try

Good to try: this is a valid backup and easy to explain, but the simplest direct solution usually reads better in an interview.

fun moveAllNegativeNumbersToBeginning(input: List<Int>): List<Int> {
    val negatives = mutableListOf<Int>()
    val nonNegatives = mutableListOf<Int>()
    for (value in input) if (value < 0) negatives.add(value) else nonNegatives.add(value)
    return negatives + nonNegatives

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Loop Best

Best: this is the clearest control-flow version and is a reliable fallback if the interviewer prefers explicit logic.

fun moveAllNegativeNumbersToBeginning(input: List<Int>): List<Int> {
    val result = input.toMutableList()
    result.sortWith(compareBy { if (it < 0) 0 else 1 })
    return result

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(1)

Stream API Good to try

Good to try: this is concise and elegant, but it is usually a second-choice answer unless the problem is clearly built for a collection pipeline.

fun moveAllNegativeNumbersToBeginning(input: List<Int>): List<Int> {
    return input.asSequence().partition { it < 0 }.let { it.first + it.second }
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(1)

Java

Basic Solution Best

Best: this is the cleanest and most direct version, and it is usually the strongest first answer in an interview.

public static List<Integer> moveAllNegativeNumbersToBeginning(List<Integer> input) {
    List<Integer> result = new ArrayList<>(input);
    result.sort(Comparator.comparingInt(value -> value < 0 ? 0 : 1));
    return result;

}

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(n)

Alternative Good to try

Good to try: this is a valid backup and easy to explain, but the simplest direct solution usually reads better in an interview.

public static List<Integer> moveAllNegativeNumbersToBeginning(List<Integer> input) {
    List<Integer> negatives = new ArrayList<>();
    List<Integer> nonNegatives = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int value : input) if (value < 0) negatives.add(value); else nonNegatives.add(value);
    negatives.addAll(nonNegatives);
    return negatives;

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

public static List<Integer> moveAllNegativeNumbersToBeginning(List<Integer> input) {
    List<Integer> result = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int value : input) if (value < 0) result.add(value);
    for (int value : input) if (value >= 0) result.add(value);
    return result;

}

Time: O(n^2)   Space: O(n)

Stream API Good to try

Good to try: this is concise and elegant, but it is usually a second-choice answer unless the problem is clearly built for a collection pipeline.

public static List<Integer> moveAllNegativeNumbersToBeginning(List<Integer> input) {
    return input.stream().sorted(Comparator.comparingInt(value -> value < 0 ? 0 : 1)).collect(Collectors.toList());

}

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(n)

Question 32 · Arrays & Lists · Easy

Find maximum and minimum in one pass

Find the maximum and minimum in a single traversal.

Example 1

Input: [4, 1, 9, 2]

Output: {max=9, min=1}

Kotlin

Basic Solution Best

Best: this is the cleanest and most direct version, and it is usually the strongest first answer in an interview.

fun findMaximumAndMinimumInOnePass(input: List<Int>): List<Int> {
    return input.filter { it > 0 }.sorted()
}

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(1)

Alternative Good to try

Good to try: this is a valid backup and easy to explain, but the simplest direct solution usually reads better in an interview.

fun findMaximumAndMinimumInOnePass(input: List<Int>): List<Int> {
    val result = mutableListOf<Int>()
    for (value in input) {
        if (value > 0) result.add(value)
    }
    result.sort()
    return result

}

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(n)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

    result.sort()
    return result

Good to try: this is concise and elegant, but it is usually a second-choice answer unless the problem is clearly built for a collection pipeline.

}

Basic Solution Good to try

Alternative Good to try

Good to try: this is a valid backup and easy to explain, but the simplest direct solution usually reads better in an interview.

        if (value > 0) result.add(value);

    Collections.sort(result);
    return result;

}

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(n)

Question 33 · Arrays & Lists · Medium

Find top 3 largest numbers

Return the three largest numbers from the array.

Example 1

Input: [5, 1, 9, 3, 7]

Output: [9, 7, 5]

Kotlin

Basic Solution Good to try

Good to try: this is readable and compact, but the stream chain is usually less explicit than the cleaner loop-based explanation when the interviewer wants exact control.

fun findTop3LargestNumbers(input: List<Int>): Int {
    return input.maxOrNull() ?: Int.MIN_VALUE
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(1)

Alternative Good to try

Good to try: this is a valid backup and easy to explain, but the simplest direct solution usually reads better in an interview.

fun findTop3LargestNumbers(input: List<Int>): Int {
    var largest = input.firstOrNull() ?: Int.MIN_VALUE
    for (value in input.drop(1)) {
        if (value > largest) largest = value
    }
    return largest

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(1)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

fun findTop3LargestNumbers(input: List<Int>): Int {
    var largest = Int.MIN_VALUE
    for (value in input) {
        if (value > largest) largest = value
    }
    return largest

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(1)

Stream API Good to try

Good to try: this is concise and elegant, but it is usually a second-choice answer unless the problem is clearly built for a collection pipeline.

fun findTop3LargestNumbers(input: List<Int>): Int {
    return input.asSequence().maxOrNull() ?: Int.MIN_VALUE
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(1)

Java

Basic Solution Best

Best: this is the cleanest and most direct version, and it is usually the strongest first answer in an interview.

public static int findTop3LargestNumbers(List<Integer> input) {
    return Collections.max(input);
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(1)

Alternative Good to try

Good to try: this is a valid backup and easy to explain, but the simplest direct solution usually reads better in an interview.

public static int findTop3LargestNumbers(List<Integer> input) {
    int largest = input.get(0);
    for (int value : input) {
        if (value > largest) largest = value;
    }
    return largest;

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(1)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

public static int findTop3LargestNumbers(List<Integer> input) {
    int largest = Integer.MIN_VALUE;
    for (int value : input) {
        if (value > largest) largest = value;
    }
    return largest;

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(1)

Stream API Good to try

Good to try: this is concise and elegant, but it is usually a second-choice answer unless the problem is clearly built for a collection pipeline.

public static int findTop3LargestNumbers(List<Integer> input) {
    return input.stream().max(Integer::compareTo).orElse(Integer.MIN_VALUE);
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(1)

Question 34 · Arrays & Lists · Easy

Find elements greater than X

Get all values greater than a threshold X.

Example 1

Input: numbers = [2, 8, 5, 10], x = 6

Output: [8, 10]

Kotlin

Basic Solution Best

Best: this is the cleanest and most direct version, and it is usually the strongest first answer in an interview.

fun findElementsGreaterThanX(input: List<Int>): List<Int> {
    return input.filter { it > 0 }.sorted()
}

Question 36 · HashMap / HashSet · Easy

Count character frequency

Count the frequency of each character.

Example 1

Input: [1, 2, 2, 3]

Output: {1=1, 2=2, 3=1}

Kotlin

Basic Solution Good to try

Good to try: this is readable and compact, but the stream chain is usually less explicit than the cleaner loop-based explanation when the interviewer wants exact control.

fun countCharacterFrequency(input: String): Map<Char, Int> {
    return input.groupingBy { it }.eachCount()
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Alternative Good to try

Good to try: this is a valid backup and easy to explain, but the simplest direct solution usually reads better in an interview.

fun countCharacterFrequency(input: String): Map<Char, Int> {
    val counts = mutableMapOf<Char, Int>()
    for (char in input) {
        counts[char] = counts.getOrDefault(char, 0) + 1
    }
    return counts

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

fun countCharacterFrequency(input: String): Map<Char, Int> {
    val counts = mutableMapOf<Char, Int>()
    for (char in input) {
        if (counts.containsKey(char)) {
            counts[char] = counts[char]!! + 1
        } else {
            counts[char] = 1
        }
    }
    return counts

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Stream API Good to try

Good to try: this is concise and elegant, but it is usually a second-choice answer unless the problem is clearly built for a collection pipeline.

fun countCharacterFrequency(input: String): Map<Char, Int> {
    return input.asSequence().groupingBy { it }.eachCount()
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Java

Basic Solution Avoid unless needed

Good to try: this is readable and compact, but the stream chain is usually less explicit than the cleaner loop-based explanation when the interviewer wants exact control.

public static Map<Character, Integer> countCharacterFrequency(String input) {
    return input.chars().mapToObj(c -> (char) c).collect(Collectors.groupingBy(c -> c, LinkedHashMap::new, Collectors.summingInt(c -> 1)));
 }

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Alternative Avoid unless needed

Best: this keeps the core idea simple and readable without overcomplicating the logic.

    return input.chars().mapToObj(c -> (char) c).collect(Collectors.groupingBy(c -> c, LinkedHashMap::new, Collectors.summingInt(c -> 1)));
 }

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Best: this is the clearest control-flow version and is a reliable fallback if the interviewer prefers explicit logic.

 }

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Question 38 · HashMap / HashSet · Easy

Find duplicate characters

Find characters that appear more than once.

Example 1

Input: "programming"

Output: [r, g, m]

Kotlin

Basic Solution Best

Best: this is the cleanest and most direct version, and it is usually the strongest first answer in an interview.

fun findDuplicateCharacters(input: String): List<Char> {
    val seen = mutableSetOf<Char>()
    val duplicates = mutableListOf<Char>()
    for (char in input) {
        if (!seen.add(char) && char !in duplicates) duplicates.add(char)
    }
    return duplicates

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Alternative Best

Best: this keeps the core idea simple and readable without overcomplicating the logic.

fun findDuplicateCharacters(input: String): List<Char> {
    return input.groupingBy { it }.eachCount().filterValues { it > 1 }.keys.toList()
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

fun findDuplicateCharacters(input: String): List<Char> {
    val counts = mutableMapOf<Char, Int>()
    for (char in input) counts[char] = (counts[char] ?: 0) + 1
    return input.filter { counts[it]!! > 1 }.distinct()

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Stream API Good to try

Good to try: this is concise and elegant, but it is usually a second-choice answer unless the problem is clearly built for a collection pipeline.

fun findDuplicateCharacters(input: String): List<Char> {
    return input.asSequence().groupingBy { it }.eachCount().filterValues { it > 1 }.keys.toList()
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Java

Basic Solution Best

Best: this is the cleanest and most direct version, and it is usually the strongest first answer in an interview.

public static List<Character> findDuplicateCharacters(String input) {
    Set<Character> seen = new HashSet<>();
    List<Character> duplicates = new ArrayList<>();
    for (char c : input.toCharArray()) {
        if (!seen.add(c) && !duplicates.contains(c)) duplicates.add(c);
    }
    return duplicates;

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Alternative Good to try

Good to try: this is a valid backup and easy to explain, but the simplest direct solution usually reads better in an interview.

public static List<Character> findDuplicateCharacters(String input) {
    Map<Character, Integer> counts = new LinkedHashMap<>();
    for (char c : input.toCharArray()) counts.put(c, counts.getOrDefault(c, 0) + 1);
    List<Character> duplicates = new ArrayList<>();
    for (char c : input.toCharArray()) if (counts.get(c) > 1 && !duplicates.contains(c)) duplicates.add(c);
    return duplicates;

}

Time: O(n^2)   Space: O(n)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

public static List<Character> findDuplicateCharacters(String input) {
    List<Character> duplicates = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < input.length(); i++) {
        char c = input.charAt(i);
        if (input.indexOf(c) != input.lastIndexOf(c) && !duplicates.contains(c)) duplicates.add(c);
    }
    return duplicates;

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Stream API Avoid unless needed

Avoid unless needed: this flatMap/flatten style is clever but often harder to explain and reason about than a straightforward loop or direct collection approach.

public static List<Character> findDuplicateCharacters(String input) {
    Map<Character, Long> counts = input.chars().mapToObj(c -> (char) c).collect(Collectors.groupingBy(c -> c, LinkedHashMap::new, Collectors.counting()));
    return input.chars().mapToObj(c -> (char) c).filter(c -> counts.get(c) > 1).distinct().collect(Collectors.toList());

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Question 40 · HashMap / HashSet · Medium

First non-repeating character

Return the first character with count 1.

Example 1

Input: "swiss"

Output: w

Kotlin

Basic Solution Good to try

Good to try: this is readable and compact, but the stream chain is usually less explicit than the cleaner loop-based explanation when the interviewer wants exact control.

fun firstNonRepeatingCharacter(input: String): Char {
    return input.groupingBy { it }.eachCount().let { counts -> input.first { counts[it] == 1 } }
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Alternative Best

Best: this keeps the core idea simple and readable without overcomplicating the logic.

fun firstNonRepeatingCharacter(input: String): Char {
    val counts = input.groupingBy { it }.eachCount()
    return input.firstOrNull { counts[it] == 1 } ?: '\u0000'
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

fun firstNonRepeatingCharacter(input: String): Char {
    val counts = mutableMapOf<Char, Int>()
    for (char in input) counts[char] = (counts[char] ?: 0) + 1
    for (char in input) if (counts[char] == 1) return char
    return '\u0000'

}

Time: O(n^2)   Space: O(n)

Stream API Good to try

Good to try: this is concise and elegant, but it is usually a second-choice answer unless the problem is clearly built for a collection pipeline.

    return input.asSequence().groupingBy { it }.eachCount().let { counts -> input.first { counts[it] == 1 } }
}

    for (char in input) {
        if (char in seen) return char
        seen.add(char)
    }
    return '\u0000'

}

Question 49 · HashMap / HashSet · Easy

Two Sum

Find indices of two numbers that add up to the target value.

Example 1

Input: numbers = [2, 7, 11, 15], target = 9

Output: [0, 1]

Kotlin

Basic Solution Best

Best: this is the cleanest and most direct version, and it is usually the strongest first answer in an interview.

fun twoSum(numbers: IntArray, target: Int): IntArray {
    val seen = mutableMapOf<Int, Int>()
    for ((index, value) in numbers.withIndex()) {
        val complement = target - value
        if (seen.containsKey(complement)) {
            return intArrayOf(seen[complement]!!, index)
        }
        seen[value] = index
    }
    return intArrayOf()

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Alternative Good to try

Good to try: this is a valid backup and easy to explain, but the simplest direct solution usually reads better in an interview.

fun twoSum(numbers: IntArray, target: Int): IntArray {
    val seen = hashMapOf<Int, Int>()
    for (index in numbers.indices) {
        val needed = target - numbers[index]
        if (seen.containsKey(needed)) {
            return intArrayOf(seen[needed]!!, index)
        }
        seen[numbers[index]] = index
    }
    return intArrayOf()

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

fun twoSum(numbers: IntArray, target: Int): IntArray {
    val seen = mutableMapOf<Int, Int>()
    for (i in numbers.indices) {
        val wanted = target - numbers[i]
        for (j in i + 1 until numbers.size) {
            if (numbers[j] == wanted) return intArrayOf(i, j)
        }
        seen[numbers[i]] = i
    }
    return intArrayOf()

}

Time: O(n^2)   Space: O(n)

Stream API Good to try

Good to try: this is concise and elegant, but it is usually a second-choice answer unless the problem is clearly built for a collection pipeline.

fun twoSum(numbers: IntArray, target: Int): IntArray {
    return numbers.indices.asSequence()
        .mapNotNull { i ->
            val diff = target - numbers[i]
            val j = numbers.indices.firstOrNull { idx -> idx != i && numbers[idx] == diff }
            if (j != null) intArrayOf(i, j) else null
        }
        .firstOrNull() ?: intArrayOf()
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Java

Basic Solution Best

Best: this is the cleanest and most direct version, and it is usually the strongest first answer in an interview.

public static int[] twoSum(int[] numbers, int target) {
    Map<Integer, Integer> seen = new HashMap<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < numbers.length; i++) {
        int complement = target - numbers[i];
        if (seen.containsKey(complement)) {
            return new int[] { seen.get(complement), i };
        }
        seen.put(numbers[i], i);
    }
    return new int[] {};

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Alternative Good to try

Good to try: this is a valid backup and easy to explain, but the simplest direct solution usually reads better in an interview.

public static int[] twoSum(int[] numbers, int target) {
    Map<Integer, Integer> indexByValue = new HashMap<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < numbers.length; i++) {
        int needed = target - numbers[i];
        if (indexByValue.containsKey(needed)) {
            return new int[] { indexByValue.get(needed), i };
        }
        indexByValue.put(numbers[i], i);
    }
    return new int[] {};

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

public static int[] twoSum(int[] numbers, int target) {
    for (int i = 0; i < numbers.length; i++) {
        for (int j = i + 1; j < numbers.length; j++) {
            if (numbers[i] + numbers[j] == target) {
                return new int[] { i, j };
            }
        }
    }
    return new int[] {};

}

Time: O(n^2)   Space: O(1)

Stream API Best

Good to try: this version is compact and readable, although a simpler loop or direct implementation is often the stronger first answer.

public static int[] twoSum(int[] numbers, int target) {
    Map<Integer, Integer> seen = new HashMap<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < numbers.length; i++) {
        int needed = target - numbers[i];
        if (seen.containsKey(needed)) {
            return new int[] { seen.get(needed), i };
        }
        seen.put(numbers[i], i);
    }
    return new int[] {};

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Alternative Avoid unless needed

Question 54 · Sorting Problems · Easy

Sort numbers ascending

Sort a numeric list in ascending order.

Example 1

Input: [5, 1, 4, 2]

Output: [1, 2, 4, 5]

Kotlin

Basic Solution Best

Best: this is the cleanest and most direct version, and it is usually the strongest first answer in an interview.

fun sortNumbersAscending(input: List<Int>): List<Int> {
    return input.filter { it > 0 }.sorted()
}

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(1)

Alternative Good to try

Good to try: this is a valid backup and easy to explain, but the simplest direct solution usually reads better in an interview.

fun sortNumbersAscending(input: List<Int>): List<Int> {
    val result = mutableListOf<Int>()
    for (value in input) {
        if (value > 0) result.add(value)
    }
    result.sort()
    return result

}

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(n)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

fun sortNumbersAscending(input: List<Int>): List<Int> {
    val result = mutableListOf<Int>()
    for (value in input) {
        if (value > 0) result.add(value)
    }
    result.sort()
    return result

}

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(n)

Stream API Good to try

Good to try: this is concise and elegant, but it is usually a second-choice answer unless the problem is clearly built for a collection pipeline.

fun sortNumbersAscending(input: List<Int>): List<Int> {
    return input.asSequence().filter { it > 0 }.sorted().toList()
}

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(n)

Java

Basic Solution Good to try

Good to try: this is readable and compact, but the stream chain is usually less explicit than the cleaner loop-based explanation when the interviewer wants exact control.

    return input.stream().filter(value -> value > 0).sorted().toList();
}

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(n)

Alternative Good to try

Good to try: this is a valid backup and easy to explain, but the simplest direct solution usually reads better in an interview.

public static List<Integer> sortNumbersAscending(List<Integer> input) {
    List<Integer> result = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int value : input) {
        if (value > 0) result.add(value);
    }
    Collections.sort(result);
    return result;

}

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(n)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

    return result;

}

Good to try: this is concise and elegant, but it is usually a second-choice answer unless the problem is clearly built for a collection pipeline.

Question 56 · Sorting Problems · Easy

Sort strings alphabetically

Kotlin

Basic Solution Best

Best: this is the cleanest and most direct version, and it is usually the strongest first answer in an interview.

fun sortStringsAlphabetically(input: String): String {
    return input.split(Regex("\s+")).sorted().joinToString(" ")
}

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(n)

Alternative Best

Best: this keeps the core idea simple and readable without overcomplicating the logic.

fun sortStringsAlphabetically(input: String): String {

      return words.sorted().joinToString(" ")
    }

Loop Best

Best: this is the clearest control-flow version and is a reliable fallback if the interviewer prefers explicit logic.

      return words.joinToString(" ")
    }

Best: this keeps the core idea simple and readable without overcomplicating the logic.

Loop Best

Best: this is the clearest control-flow version and is a reliable fallback if the interviewer prefers explicit logic.

public static String sortStringsAlphabetically(String input) {

Good to try: this is concise and elegant, but it is usually a second-choice answer unless the problem is clearly built for a collection pipeline.

Question 59 · Sorting Problems · Easy

Find second largest using sorting

Find the second largest value using a sorted view.

Example 1

Input: [5, 1, 9, 3]

Output: 5

Kotlin

Basic Solution Best

Best: this is the cleanest and most direct version, and it is usually the strongest first answer in an interview.

fun findSecondLargestUsingSorting(input: List<Int>): Int {
    return input.sortedDescending().distinct()[1]
}

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(1)

Alternative Best

Best: this keeps the core idea simple and readable without overcomplicating the logic.

fun findSecondLargestUsingSorting(input: List<Int>): Int {
    val distinct = input.distinct().sortedDescending()
    return if (distinct.size >= 2) distinct[1] else distinct.firstOrNull() ?: Int.MIN_VALUE

}

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(1)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

fun findSecondLargestUsingSorting(input: List<Int>): Int {
    var largest = Int.MIN_VALUE
    var secondLargest = Int.MIN_VALUE
    for (value in input) {
        if (value > largest) {
            secondLargest = largest
            largest = value
        } else if (value > secondLargest && value != largest) {
            secondLargest = value
        }
    }
    return secondLargest

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(1)

Stream API Good to try

Good to try: this is concise and elegant, but it is usually a second-choice answer unless the problem is clearly built for a collection pipeline.

fun findSecondLargestUsingSorting(input: List<Int>): Int {
    return input.asSequence().sortedDescending().distinct().drop(1).firstOrNull() ?: Int.MIN_VALUE
}

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(1)

Java

Basic Solution Good to try

Good to try: this is readable and compact, but the stream chain is usually less explicit than the cleaner loop-based explanation when the interviewer wants exact control.

public static List<Integer> findSecondLargestUsingSorting(List<Integer> input) {
    return input.stream().filter(value -> value > 0).sorted().toList();
}

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(n)

Alternative Good to try

Good to try: this is a valid backup and easy to explain, but the simplest direct solution usually reads better in an interview.

public static List<Integer> findSecondLargestUsingSorting(List<Integer> input) {
    List<Integer> result = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int value : input) {
        if (value > 0) result.add(value);
    }
    Collections.sort(result);
    return result;

}

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(n)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

public static List<Integer> findSecondLargestUsingSorting(List<Integer> input) {

    for (int value : input) {
        if (value > 0) result.add(value);
    }
    Collections.sort(result);
    return result;

}

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(n)

Stream API Good to try

Good to try: this is concise and elegant, but it is usually a second-choice answer unless the problem is clearly built for a collection pipeline.

    return input.stream().filter(value -> value > 0).sorted().collect(Collectors.toList());
}

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(n)

Question 60 · Sorting Problems · Medium

Find kth largest element

Return the k-th largest element from a collection.

Example 1

Input: numbers = [3, 2, 1, 5, 6, 4], k = 2

Output: 5

Kotlin

Basic Solution Best

Best: this is the cleanest and most direct version, and it is usually the strongest first answer in an interview.

Good to try: this is a valid backup and easy to explain, but the simplest direct solution usually reads better in an interview.

fun findKthLargestElement(input: List<Int>): List<Int> {
    val result = mutableListOf<Int>()
    for (value in input) {
        if (value > 0) result.add(value)
    }
    result.sort()
    return result

}

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(n)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

Question 61 · Sorting Problems · Easy

Sort characters in a String

Sort the characters inside a string.

Example 1

Input: "dcba"

Output: "abcd"

Kotlin

Basic Solution Best

Best: this is the cleanest and most direct version, and it is usually the strongest first answer in an interview.

fun sortCharactersInAString(input: String): String {
    return input.toCharArray().sorted().joinToString("")
}

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(n)

Alternative Best

Best: this keeps the core idea simple and readable without overcomplicating the logic.

fun sortCharactersInAString(input: String): String {
      val chars = input.toCharArray()
      return chars.sorted().joinToString("")
    }

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(n)

Loop Best

Best: this is the clearest control-flow version and is a reliable fallback if the interviewer prefers explicit logic.

fun sortCharactersInAString(input: String): String {
      val chars = input.toCharArray()
      val ordered = chars.sorted()
      return ordered.joinToString("")
    }

Question 82 · Kotlin map, filter, distinct, groupBy · Easy

Remove duplicate strings

Remove repeated strings while preserving order.

Example 1

Input: ["a", "b", "a"]

Output: ["a", "b"]

Kotlin

Basic Solution Best

Best: this is the cleanest and most direct version, and it is usually the strongest first answer in an interview.

fun removeDuplicateStrings(input: List<String>): List<String> {
    return input.distinct()
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(1)

Alternative Best

Best: this keeps the core idea simple and readable without overcomplicating the logic.

fun removeDuplicateStrings(input: List<String>): List<String> {
      return input.distinct()
    }

Time: O(n)   Space: O(1)

Loop Best

Best: this is the clearest control-flow version and is a reliable fallback if the interviewer prefers explicit logic.

fun removeDuplicateStrings(input: List<String>): List<String> {
      val seen = mutableSetOf<String>()
      return input.filter { seen.add(it) }
    }

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Stream API Best

Good to try: this version is compact and readable, although a simpler loop or direct implementation is often the stronger first answer.

fun removeDuplicateStrings(input: List<String>): List<String> {
    return input.distinct()
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(1)

Question 83 · Kotlin map, filter, distinct, groupBy · Easy

Find unique characters

Get the set of unique characters from a string.

Example 1

Input: "banana"

Output: [b, n]

Kotlin

Basic Solution Best

Best: this is the cleanest and most direct version, and it is usually the strongest first answer in an interview.

fun findUniqueCharacters(input: String): List<Char> {
    return input.filter { char -> input.count { it == char } == 1 }.distinct()
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(1)

Alternative Best

Best: this keeps the core idea simple and readable without overcomplicating the logic.

fun findUniqueCharacters(input: String): List<Char> {
    val counts = input.groupingBy { it }.eachCount()
    return input.filter { counts[it] == 1 }.distinct()
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

fun findUniqueCharacters(input: String): List<Char> {
    val counts = mutableMapOf<Char, Int>()
    for (char in input) counts[char] = (counts[char] ?: 0) + 1
    return input.filter { counts[it] == 1 }.distinct()

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Stream API Good to try

Good to try: this is concise and elegant, but it is usually a second-choice answer unless the problem is clearly built for a collection pipeline.

fun findUniqueCharacters(input: String): List<Char> {
    return input.asSequence().filter { char -> input.count { it == char } == 1 }.distinct().toList()
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Java

Basic Solution Good to try

Good to try: this is readable and compact, but the stream chain is usually less explicit than the cleaner loop-based explanation when the interviewer wants exact control.

public static List<Character> findUniqueCharacters(String input) { return input.chars().mapToObj(c -> (char) c).distinct().collect(Collectors.toList()); }

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Alternative Best

Best: this keeps the core idea simple and readable without overcomplicating the logic.

public static List<Character> findUniqueCharacters(String input) { return input.chars().mapToObj(c -> (char) c).distinct().collect(Collectors.toList()); }

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Loop Best

Best: this is the clearest control-flow version and is a reliable fallback if the interviewer prefers explicit logic.

Question 91 · Common Interview Problems · Easy

Two Sum

Solve the classic pair-sum problem with a map.

Example 1

Input: numbers = [2, 7, 11, 15], target = 9

Output: [0, 1]

Kotlin

Basic Solution Best

Best: this is the cleanest and most direct version, and it is usually the strongest first answer in an interview.

fun twoSum(numbers: IntArray, target: Int): IntArray {
    val seen = mutableMapOf<Int, Int>()
    for ((index, value) in numbers.withIndex()) {
        val complement = target - value
        if (seen.containsKey(complement)) {
            return intArrayOf(seen[complement]!!, index)
        }
        seen[value] = index
    }
    return intArrayOf()

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Alternative Good to try

Good to try: this is a valid backup and easy to explain, but the simplest direct solution usually reads better in an interview.

fun twoSum(numbers: IntArray, target: Int): IntArray {
    val seen = hashMapOf<Int, Int>()
    for (index in numbers.indices) {
        val needed = target - numbers[index]
        if (seen.containsKey(needed)) {
            return intArrayOf(seen[needed]!!, index)
        }
        seen[numbers[index]] = index
    }
    return intArrayOf()

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

fun twoSum(numbers: IntArray, target: Int): IntArray {
    val seen = mutableMapOf<Int, Int>()
    for (i in numbers.indices) {
        val wanted = target - numbers[i]
        for (j in i + 1 until numbers.size) {
            if (numbers[j] == wanted) return intArrayOf(i, j)
        }
        seen[numbers[i]] = i
    }
    return intArrayOf()

}

Time: O(n^2)   Space: O(n)

Stream API Good to try

Good to try: this is concise and elegant, but it is usually a second-choice answer unless the problem is clearly built for a collection pipeline.

fun twoSum(numbers: IntArray, target: Int): IntArray {
    return numbers.indices.asSequence()
        .mapNotNull { i ->
            val diff = target - numbers[i]
            val j = numbers.indices.firstOrNull { idx -> idx != i && numbers[idx] == diff }
            if (j != null) intArrayOf(i, j) else null
        }
        .firstOrNull() ?: intArrayOf()
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Java

Basic Solution Best

Best: this is the cleanest and most direct version, and it is usually the strongest first answer in an interview.

public static int[] twoSum(int[] numbers, int target) {
    Map<Integer, Integer> seen = new HashMap<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < numbers.length; i++) {
        int complement = target - numbers[i];
        if (seen.containsKey(complement)) {
            return new int[] { seen.get(complement), i };
        }
        seen.put(numbers[i], i);
    }
    return new int[] {};

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Alternative Good to try

Good to try: this is a valid backup and easy to explain, but the simplest direct solution usually reads better in an interview.

public static int[] twoSum(int[] numbers, int target) {
    Map<Integer, Integer> indexByValue = new HashMap<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < numbers.length; i++) {
        int needed = target - numbers[i];
        if (indexByValue.containsKey(needed)) {
            return new int[] { indexByValue.get(needed), i };
        }
        indexByValue.put(numbers[i], i);
    }
    return new int[] {};

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

public static int[] twoSum(int[] numbers, int target) {
    for (int i = 0; i < numbers.length; i++) {
        for (int j = i + 1; j < numbers.length; j++) {
            if (numbers[i] + numbers[j] == target) {
                return new int[] { i, j };
            }
        }
    }
    return new int[] {};

}

Time: O(n^2)   Space: O(1)

Stream API Best

Good to try: this version is compact and readable, although a simpler loop or direct implementation is often the stronger first answer.

public static int[] twoSum(int[] numbers, int target) {
    Map<Integer, Integer> seen = new HashMap<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < numbers.length; i++) {
        int needed = target - numbers[i];
        if (seen.containsKey(needed)) {
            return new int[] { seen.get(needed), i };
        }
        seen.put(numbers[i], i);
    }
    return new int[] {};

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)
