Question 94 · Common Interview Problems · Easy

First Unique Character

Find the first character that appears only once.

Example 1

Input: "leetcode"

Output: "l"

Kotlin

Basic Solution Good to try

Good to try: this is readable and compact, but the stream chain is usually less explicit than the cleaner loop-based explanation when the interviewer wants exact control.

fun firstUniqueCharacter(input: String): Char {
    return input.groupingBy { it }.eachCount().let { counts -> input.first { counts[it] == 1 } }
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Alternative Best

Best: this keeps the core idea simple and readable without overcomplicating the logic.

fun firstUniqueCharacter(input: String): Char {
    val counts = input.groupingBy { it }.eachCount()
    return input.firstOrNull { counts[it] == 1 } ?: '\u0000'
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

fun firstUniqueCharacter(input: String): Char {
    val counts = mutableMapOf<Char, Int>()
    for (char in input) counts[char] = (counts[char] ?: 0) + 1
    for (char in input) if (counts[char] == 1) return char
    return '\u0000'

}

Time: O(n^2)   Space: O(n)

Stream API Good to try

Good to try: this is concise and elegant, but it is usually a second-choice answer unless the problem is clearly built for a collection pipeline.

fun firstUniqueCharacter(input: String): Char {
    return input.asSequence().groupingBy { it }.eachCount().let { counts -> input.first { counts[it] == 1 } }
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Java

Basic Solution Good to try

Good to try: this is readable and compact, but the stream chain is usually less explicit than the cleaner loop-based explanation when the interviewer wants exact control.

public static List<Character> firstUniqueCharacter(String input) { return input.chars().mapToObj(c -> (char) c).distinct().collect(Collectors.toList()); }

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Best: this keeps the core idea simple and readable without overcomplicating the logic.

Question 96 · Common Interview Problems · Easy

Intersection of Two Arrays

Question 100 · Common Interview Problems · Easy

Valid Parentheses

Check whether parentheses are balanced.

Example 1

Input: "([])"

Output: true

Kotlin

Basic Solution Best

Best: this is the cleanest and most direct version, and it is usually the strongest first answer in an interview.

fun validParentheses(input: String): Boolean {
    val stack = ArrayDeque<Char>()
    val pairs = mapOf(')' to '(', '}' to '{', ']' to '[')
    for (char in input) {
        if (char in "([{") stack.addLast(char)
        else if (pairs[char] != stack.removeLastOrNull()) return false
    }
    return stack.isEmpty()

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(1)

Alternative Good to try

Good to try: this is a valid backup and easy to explain, but the simplest direct solution usually reads better in an interview.

fun validParentheses(input: String): Boolean {
    val stack = mutableListOf<Char>()
    val pairs = mapOf(')' to '(', '}' to '{', ']' to '[')
    for (char in input) {
        if (char in "([{" ) stack.add(char)
        else if (pairs[char] == stack.lastOrNull()) stack.removeAt(stack.lastIndex)
        else return false
    }
    return stack.isEmpty()

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

fun validParentheses(input: String): Boolean {
    val stack = ArrayDeque<Char>()
    for (char in input) {
        when (char) {
            '(' -> stack.addLast(char)
            '[' -> stack.addLast(char)
            '{' -> stack.addLast(char)
            ')' -> if (stack.removeLastOrNull() != '(') return false
            ']' -> if (stack.removeLastOrNull() != '[') return false
            '}' -> if (stack.removeLastOrNull() != '{') return false
        }
    }
    return stack.isEmpty()

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(1)

Stream API Good to try

Good to try: this is concise and elegant, but it is usually a second-choice answer unless the problem is clearly built for a collection pipeline.

fun validParentheses(input: String): Boolean {
    val stack = ArrayDeque<Char>()
      val valid = input.asSequence().all { char ->
        when (char) {
          '(' -> {
            stack.addLast(char)
            true
          }
          '[' -> {
            stack.addLast(char)
            true
          }
          '{' -> {
            stack.addLast(char)
            true
          }
          ')' -> stack.removeLastOrNull() == '('
          ']' -> stack.removeLastOrNull() == '['
          '}' -> stack.removeLastOrNull() == '{'
          else -> true
        }
      }
      return valid && stack.isEmpty()

    }

Time: O(n)   Space: O(1)

Java

Basic Solution Best

Best: this is the cleanest and most direct version, and it is usually the strongest first answer in an interview.

public static boolean validParentheses(String input) {
    Deque<Character> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();
    Map<Character, Character> pairs = Map.of(')', '(', '}', '{', ']', '[');
    for (char c : input.toCharArray()) {
        if (c == '(' || c == '[' || c == '{') stack.push(c);
        else if (stack.isEmpty() || stack.pop() != pairs.get(c)) return false;
    }
    return stack.isEmpty();

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Alternative Good to try

Good to try: this is a valid backup and easy to explain, but the simplest direct solution usually reads better in an interview.

public static boolean validParentheses(String input) {
    Deque<Character> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();
    for (char c : input.toCharArray()) {
        if (c == '(' || c == '[' || c == '{') stack.push(c);
        else if (c == ')' && (stack.isEmpty() || stack.pop() != '(')) return false;
        else if (c == ']' && (stack.isEmpty() || stack.pop() != '[')) return false;
        else if (c == '}' && (stack.isEmpty() || stack.pop() != '{')) return false;
    }
    return stack.isEmpty();

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

public static boolean validParentheses(String input) {
    Deque<Character> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();
    for (char c : input.toCharArray()) {
        switch (c) {
            case '(':
            case '[':
            case '{':
                stack.push(c);
                break;
            case ')':
                if (stack.isEmpty() || stack.pop() != '(') return false;
                break;
            case ']':
                if (stack.isEmpty() || stack.pop() != '[') return false;
                break;
            case '}':
                if (stack.isEmpty() || stack.pop() != '{') return false;
                break;
            default:
                break;
        }
    }
    return stack.isEmpty();

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Stream API Best

Good to try: this version is compact and readable, although a simpler loop or direct implementation is often the stronger first answer.

public static boolean validParentheses(String input) {
    Deque<Character> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();
    return input.chars().allMatch(ch -> {
        if (ch == '(' || ch == '[' || ch == '{') {
            stack.push((char) ch);
            return true;
        }
        if (ch == ')' && (stack.isEmpty() || stack.pop() != '(')) return false;
        if (ch == ']' && (stack.isEmpty() || stack.pop() != '[')) return false;
        if (ch == '}' && (stack.isEmpty() || stack.pop() != '{')) return false;
        return true;
    }) && stack.isEmpty();

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(1)

Question 101 · Common Interview Problems · Medium

Reverse Linked List

Reverse the order of nodes in a singly linked list.

Example 1

Input: 1 -> 2 -> 3 -> null

Output: 3 -> 2 -> 1 -> null

Kotlin

Basic Solution Best

Best: this is the cleanest and most direct version, and it is usually the strongest first answer in an interview.

fun reverseLinkedList(input: List<Int>): List<Int> {
    return input.asReversed()
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(1)

Alternative Good to try

Good to try: this is a valid backup and easy to explain, but the simplest direct solution usually reads better in an interview.

fun reverseLinkedList(input: List<Int>): List<Int> {
    val reversed = mutableListOf<Int>()
      for (value in input.asReversed()) reversed.add(value)
      return reversed

    }

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

fun reverseLinkedList(input: List<Int>): List<Int> {
    val result = mutableListOf<Int>()
      for (index in input.indices.reversed()) result.add(input[index])
      return result

    }

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Stream API Best

Good to try: this version is compact and readable, although a simpler loop or direct implementation is often the stronger first answer.

fun reverseLinkedList(input: List<Int>): List<Int> {
    return input.asReversed()
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(1)

Java

Basic Solution Best

public static List<Integer> reverseLinkedList(List<Integer> input) { List<Integer> reversed = new ArrayList<>(input); Collections.reverse(reversed); return reversed; }

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Alternative Good to try

Good to try: this is a valid backup and easy to explain, but the simplest direct solution usually reads better in an interview.

public static List<Integer> reverseLinkedList(List<Integer> input) {
    List<Integer> reversed = new ArrayList<>(); for (int i = input.size() - 1; i >= 0; i--) reversed.add(input.get(i)); return reversed;
 }

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

Question 103 · Common Interview Problems · Easy

Best Time to Buy/Sell Stock

Find the maximum profit from a single stock transaction.

Example 1

Input: [7, 1, 5, 3, 6, 4]

Output: 5

Kotlin

Basic Solution Best

Best: this is the cleanest and most direct version, and it is usually the strongest first answer in an interview.

fun bestTimeToBuySellStock(prices: IntArray): Int {
    if (prices.isEmpty()) return 0
    var minPrice = prices[0]
    var bestProfit = 0
    for (price in prices.drop(1)) {
        minPrice = minOf(minPrice, price)
        bestProfit = maxOf(bestProfit, price - minPrice)
    }
    return bestProfit

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Alternative Good to try

Good to try: this is a valid backup and easy to explain, but the simplest direct solution usually reads better in an interview.

fun bestTimeToBuySellStock(prices: IntArray): Int {
    var minPrice = Int.MAX_VALUE
    var maxProfit = 0
    for (price in prices) {
        minPrice = minOf(minPrice, price)
        maxProfit = maxOf(maxProfit, price - minPrice)
    }
    return maxProfit

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

fun bestTimeToBuySellStock(prices: IntArray): Int {
    var minPrice = Int.MAX_VALUE
    var maxProfit = 0
    for (price in prices) {
        if (price < minPrice) minPrice = price
        else maxProfit = maxOf(maxProfit, price - minPrice)
    }
    return maxProfit

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Stream API Good to try

Good to try: this is concise and elegant, but it is usually a second-choice answer unless the problem is clearly built for a collection pipeline.

fun bestTimeToBuySellStock(prices: IntArray): Int {
    return prices.asSequence().fold(Pair(Int.MAX_VALUE, 0)) { state, price ->
    val minPrice = minOf(state.first, price)
    val maxProfit = maxOf(state.second, price - minPrice)
    minPrice to maxProfit
}.second
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Java

Basic Solution Good to try

Good to try: this is readable and compact, but the stream chain is usually less explicit than the cleaner loop-based explanation when the interviewer wants exact control.

public static List<Integer> bestTimeToBuySellStock(List<Integer> input) {
    return input.stream().filter(value -> value > 0).sorted().toList();
}

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(n)

Alternative Good to try

Good to try: this is a valid backup and easy to explain, but the simplest direct solution usually reads better in an interview.

public static List<Integer> bestTimeToBuySellStock(List<Integer> input) {
    List<Integer> result = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int value : input) {
        if (value > 0) result.add(value);
    }
    Collections.sort(result);
    return result;

}

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(n)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

public static List<Integer> bestTimeToBuySellStock(List<Integer> input) {
    List<Integer> result = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int value : input) {
        if (value > 0) result.add(value);
    }
    Collections.sort(result);
    return result;

}

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(n)

Stream API Good to try

Good to try: this is concise and elegant, but it is usually a second-choice answer unless the problem is clearly built for a collection pipeline.

public static List<Integer> bestTimeToBuySellStock(List<Integer> input) {
    return input.stream().filter(value -> value > 0).sorted().collect(Collectors.toList());
}

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(n)

Question 104 · Common Interview Problems · Medium

Maximum Subarray

Find the contiguous subarray with the largest sum.

Example 1

Input: [-2, 1, -3, 4, -1, 2, 1, -5, 4]

Output: 6

Kotlin

Basic Solution Best

Best: this is the cleanest and most direct version, and it is usually the strongest first answer in an interview.

fun maximumSubarray(prices: IntArray): Int {
    if (prices.isEmpty()) return 0
    var minPrice = prices[0]
    var bestProfit = 0
    for (price in prices.drop(1)) {
        minPrice = minOf(minPrice, price)
        bestProfit = maxOf(bestProfit, price - minPrice)
    }
    return bestProfit

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Alternative Good to try

Good to try: this is a valid backup and easy to explain, but the simplest direct solution usually reads better in an interview.

fun maximumSubarray(prices: IntArray): Int {
    var minPrice = Int.MAX_VALUE
    var maxProfit = 0
    for (price in prices) {
        minPrice = minOf(minPrice, price)
        maxProfit = maxOf(maxProfit, price - minPrice)
    }
    return maxProfit

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

fun maximumSubarray(prices: IntArray): Int {
    var minPrice = Int.MAX_VALUE
    var maxProfit = 0
    for (price in prices) {
        if (price < minPrice) minPrice = price
        else maxProfit = maxOf(maxProfit, price - minPrice)
    }
    return maxProfit

}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Stream API Good to try

Good to try: this is concise and elegant, but it is usually a second-choice answer unless the problem is clearly built for a collection pipeline.

fun maximumSubarray(prices: IntArray): Int {
    return prices.asSequence().fold(Pair(Int.MAX_VALUE, 0)) { state, price ->
    val minPrice = minOf(state.first, price)
    val maxProfit = maxOf(state.second, price - minPrice)
    minPrice to maxProfit
}.second
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Java

Basic Solution Good to try

Good to try: this is readable and compact, but the stream chain is usually less explicit than the cleaner loop-based explanation when the interviewer wants exact control.

public static List<Integer> maximumSubarray(List<Integer> input) {
    return input.stream().filter(value -> value > 0).sorted().toList();
}

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(n)

Alternative Good to try

Good to try: this is a valid backup and easy to explain, but the simplest direct solution usually reads better in an interview.

public static List<Integer> maximumSubarray(List<Integer> input) {
    List<Integer> result = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int value : input) {
        if (value > 0) result.add(value);
    }
    Collections.sort(result);
    return result;

}

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(n)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

    return result;

Stream API Good to try

Good to try: this is concise and elegant, but it is usually a second-choice answer unless the problem is clearly built for a collection pipeline.

public static List<Integer> maximumSubarray(List<Integer> input) {
    return input.stream().filter(value -> value > 0).sorted().collect(Collectors.toList());
}

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(n)

Question 105 · Common Interview Problems · Easy

Binary Search

Search for a target value in a sorted array.

Example 1

Input: numbers = [1, 3, 5, 7], target = 5

Output: index 2

Kotlin

Basic Solution Best

Best: this is the cleanest and most direct version, and it is usually the strongest first answer in an interview.

fun binarySearch(input: List<Int>): Int {
    return input.binarySearch(input.firstOrNull() ?: 0)
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(1)

Alternative Best

Best: this keeps the core idea simple and readable without overcomplicating the logic.

fun binarySearch(input: List<Int>): Int {
    return input.binarySearch(input.firstOrNull() ?: 0)
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(1)

Loop Best

Best: this is the clearest control-flow version and is a reliable fallback if the interviewer prefers explicit logic.

Question 109 · Interview Combination Problems · Medium

Product of Array Except Self

Return the product of all elements except the current one.

Example 1

Input: [1, 2, 3, 4]

Output: [24, 12, 8, 6]

Kotlin

Basic Solution Best

Best: this is the cleanest and most direct version, and it is usually the strongest first answer in an interview.

fun productOfArrayExceptSelf(input: List<Int>): List<Int> {
    return input.filter { it > 0 }.sorted()
}

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(1)

Alternative Good to try

Good to try: this is a valid backup and easy to explain, but the simplest direct solution usually reads better in an interview.

fun productOfArrayExceptSelf(input: List<Int>): List<Int> {
    val result = mutableListOf<Int>()
    for (value in input) {
        if (value > 0) result.add(value)
    }
    result.sort()
    return result

}

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(n)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

public static List<Integer> subarraySumEqualsK(List<Integer> input) { return input.stream().distinct().sorted().collect(Collectors.toList()); }

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(n)

Question 114 · Interview Combination Problems · Medium

Longest Consecutive Sequence

Find the length of the longest consecutive sequence.

Example 1

Input: [100, 4, 200, 1, 3, 2]

Output: 4

Kotlin

Basic Solution Good to try

Good to try: this is readable and compact, but the stream chain is usually less explicit than the cleaner loop-based explanation when the interviewer wants exact control.

fun longestConsecutiveSequence(input: List<Int>): Map<Int, Int> {
    return input.groupingBy { it }.eachCount()
}

Question 116 · Interview Combination Problems · Medium

Kth Largest Element

Find the k-th largest element in a list.

Example 1

Input: numbers = [3, 2, 1, 5, 6, 4], k = 2

Output: 5

Kotlin

Basic Solution Best

Best: this is the cleanest and most direct version, and it is usually the strongest first answer in an interview.

fun kthLargestElement(input: List<Int>): List<Int> {
    return input.filter { it > 0 }.sorted()
}

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(1)

Alternative Good to try

Good to try: this is a valid backup and easy to explain, but the simplest direct solution usually reads better in an interview.

fun kthLargestElement(input: List<Int>): List<Int> {
    val result = mutableListOf<Int>()
    for (value in input) {
        if (value > 0) result.add(value)
    }
    result.sort()
    return result

}

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(n)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

fun kthLargestElement(input: List<Int>): List<Int> {
    val result = mutableListOf<Int>()
    for (value in input) {
        if (value > 0) result.add(value)
    }
    result.sort()
    return result

}

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(n)

Stream API Good to try

Good to try: this is concise and elegant, but it is usually a second-choice answer unless the problem is clearly built for a collection pipeline.

fun kthLargestElement(input: List<Int>): List<Int> {
    return input.asSequence().filter { it > 0 }.sorted().toList()
}

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(n)

Java

Basic Solution Good to try

Good to try: this is readable and compact, but the stream chain is usually less explicit than the cleaner loop-based explanation when the interviewer wants exact control.

public static List<Integer> kthLargestElement(List<Integer> input) {
    return input.stream().filter(value -> value > 0).sorted().toList();
}

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(n)

Alternative Good to try

Good to try: this is a valid backup and easy to explain, but the simplest direct solution usually reads better in an interview.

public static List<Integer> kthLargestElement(List<Integer> input) {
    List<Integer> result = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int value : input) {
        if (value > 0) result.add(value);
    }
    Collections.sort(result);
    return result;

}

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(n)

Loop Good to try

Good to try: the explicit loop is easy to reason about, which makes it a strong backup when the interviewer wants to see every step.

public static List<Integer> kthLargestElement(List<Integer> input) {
    List<Integer> result = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int value : input) {
        if (value > 0) result.add(value);
    }
    Collections.sort(result);
    return result;

}

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(n)

Stream API Good to try

Good to try: this is concise and elegant, but it is usually a second-choice answer unless the problem is clearly built for a collection pipeline.

public static List<Integer> kthLargestElement(List<Integer> input) {
    return input.stream().filter(value -> value > 0).sorted().collect(Collectors.toList());
}

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(n)

Question 117 · Interview Combination Problems · Medium

Binary Tree Traversal

Traverse a binary tree using depth-first or breadth-first order.

Example 1

Input: tree = [1, 2, 3]

Output: [1, 2, 3]

Kotlin

Basic Solution Best

Best: this is the cleanest and most direct version, and it is usually the strongest first answer in an interview.

fun binaryTreeTraversal(input: List<Int>): List<Int> {
    return input.sorted()
}

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(1)

Alternative Best

Best: this keeps the core idea simple and readable without overcomplicating the logic.

fun binaryTreeTraversal(input: List<Int>): List<Int> { return input.sorted() }

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(1)

Loop Best

Best: this is the clearest control-flow version and is a reliable fallback if the interviewer prefers explicit logic.

fun binaryTreeTraversal(input: List<Int>): List<Int> {
      val result = input.toMutableList()
      result.sort()
      return result
    }

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(1)

Stream API Best

Good to try: this version is compact and readable, although a simpler loop or direct implementation is often the stronger first answer.

fun binaryTreeTraversal(input: List<Int>): List<Int> {
    return input.sorted()
}

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(1)

Java

Basic Solution Best

Best: this is the cleanest and most direct version, and it is usually the strongest first answer in an interview.

public static List<Integer> binaryTreeTraversal(List<Integer> input) { List<Integer> ordered = new ArrayList<>(input); Collections.sort(ordered); return ordered; }

Time: O(n log n)   Space: O(n)

Alternative Best

Best: this keeps the core idea simple and readable without overcomplicating the logic.

Question 118 · Interview Combination Problems · Medium

BFS

Traverse a graph using breadth-first search.

Example 1

Input: A -> [B, C], B -> [D], C -> []

Output: [A, B, C, D]

Kotlin

Basic Solution Best

Best: this is the cleanest and most direct version, and it is usually the strongest first answer in an interview.

fun bfs(input: List<String>): List<String> {
    return input.distinct()
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(1)

Alternative Best

Best: this keeps the core idea simple and readable without overcomplicating the logic.

fun bfs(input: List<String>): List<String> { return input.distinct() }

Time: O(n)   Space: O(1)

Loop Best

Best: this is the clearest control-flow version and is a reliable fallback if the interviewer prefers explicit logic.

fun bfs(input: List<String>): List<String> {
        val seen = mutableSetOf<String>()
        return input.filter { seen.add(it) }
    }

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Stream API Good to try

Good to try: this is concise and elegant, but it is usually a second-choice answer unless the problem is clearly built for a collection pipeline.

fun bfs(input: List<String>): List<String> {
    return input.asSequence().distinct().toList()
}

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Java

Basic Solution Best

Best: this is the cleanest and most direct version, and it is usually the strongest first answer in an interview.

public static List<String> bfs(List<String> input) { return new ArrayList<>(new LinkedHashSet<>(input)); }

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Alternative Best

Best: this keeps the core idea simple and readable without overcomplicating the logic.

public static List<String> bfs(List<String> input) { return new ArrayList<>(new LinkedHashSet<>(input)); }

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Loop Best

Best: this is the clearest control-flow version and is a reliable fallback if the interviewer prefers explicit logic.

public static List<String> bfs(List<String> input) { Set<String> seen = new LinkedHashSet<>(); seen.addAll(input); return new ArrayList<>(seen); }

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)

Stream API Good to try

Good to try: this is concise and elegant, but it is usually a second-choice answer unless the problem is clearly built for a collection pipeline.

public static List<String> bfs(List<String> input) { return input.stream().distinct().collect(Collectors.toList()); }

Time: O(n)   Space: O(n)
